Here's what I found:\n\n## Positional Encoding in Transformer Architecture\n\nPositional encoding gained significant popularity through its use in the paper \"Attention Is All You Need\" by Vaswani et al., which introduced the Transformer architecture.\n\n### The Sinusoidal Approach\n\nIn the original paper introduced by Vaswani et al. 2017, the key idea is to generate a fixed and deterministic encoding for each position in a sequence by using a sinusoidal function—particularly, the sine function. The authors proposed using a combination of sine and cosine functions at different frequencies to encode position.\n\n### Why Positional Encoding is Needed\n\nAs each word in a sentence simultaneously flows through the Transformer's encoder/decoder stack, the model itself doesn't have any sense of position/order for each word. Consequently, there's still the need for a way to incorporate the order of the words into our model.\n\n### Properties of Sinusoidal Encoding\n\nThe sine and cosine functions have values in [-1, 1], which keeps the values of the positional encoding matrix in a normalized range. As the sinusoid for each position is different, you have a unique way of encoding each position. Here's a comprehensive overview of RoPE (Rotary Position Embedding), complex numbers, and frequency selection strategy:\n\n## Overview of RoPE\n\nRotary Positional Embeddings (RoPE) are a method that applies block-diagonal rotations to encode relative positional information in transformers. The key insight behind RoPE is to encode positional information by rotating the query and key vectors in the complex plane based on their absolute positions, while preserving relative positional information in the attention computation.\n\n## Complex Numbers in RoPE\n\nRoPE leverages the mathematical properties of complex numbers and rotation matrices. In 2D space, rotating a vector by angle θ can be represented as multiplication by the complex number e^(iθ) = cos(θ) + i·sin(θ). \n\nThe implementation first reshapes query and key vectors into two components per group, and then converts it into complex form. This is then multiplied with frequency tensors using multiplication of complex numbers.\n\n## Frequency Selection Strategy\n\nHigher dimension indices correspond to progressively slower clocks (longer wavelengths). The approach adds d/2 frequency components instead of fewer, filling in frequency gaps more finely: the exponent step 2/d becomes smaller, so the model sees a smoother spectrum of time scales, helpful for picking up subtle relative offsets.\n\nThe choice of the base hyperparameter for frequency calculation (θ_i = base^(-2i/d)) can influence performance and extrapolation capabilities, requiring careful tuning. The choice of base frequency (10000) is largely empirical, and there's insufficient theoretical guidance for optimal hyperparameter selection.\n\n### Recent Advances in Frequency Selection\n\nCARoPE generalizes static frequency patterns to dynamic, token- and head-specific patterns driven by the content, yielding further improvements in perplexity and training throughput. Additionally, instead of scaling the frequencies of every dimension of RoPE by a factor of 1/s, some approaches spread out the interpolation pressure across multiple dimensions by scaling high frequencies less and low frequencies more.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"a5185cdc-45b1-4607-acef-70a9adbb9d94","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:40:08.724Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"18b56575-0512-4e6a-a99d-b0c7de45256f","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

This is a crucial aspect of the design. Lower dimensions of the position vector have a very short wavelength (distance between identical points), while the values of the first 20 positions at the higher indexes are almost constant.\n\n## Rationale for This Design\n\nThe paper specifically comments on why this method was chosen describing: \"We chose the sinusoidal version because it may allow the model to extrapolate to sequence lengths longer than the ones encountered during training.\" Additionally, this function was chosen because the authors hypothesized it would allow the model to easily learn to attend by relative positions, since for any fixed offset k, PE_pos+k can be represented as a linear function of PE_pos.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"72a83db1-3ac4-4385-909b-804909113f7a","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:40:20.359Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9fa2950d-6a24-419b-98c8-a076a8fe639e","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

mathematical properties of good positional encoding:\n\n## Key Desirable Properties\n\nGood positional encodings should output a unique encoding for each time-step (word's position in a sentence), maintain consistent distance between any two time-steps across sentences with different lengths, generalize to longer sentences, have bounded values, and be deterministic.\n\n## Uniqueness\n\nUniqueness requires that the encoding produces a unique encoding vector for each position in the sequence. The frequency term (10000^{2i/d_{model}}) ensures that each position receives a unique encoding vector.\n\n## Bounded Values\n\nThe values of sine and cosine functions are always between -1 and 1, ensuring that the positional encodings don't grow or shrink excessively for very long sequences, which helps maintain numerical stability.\n\n## Relative Position Sensitivity\n\nSinusoidal positional encoding allows the model to attend relative positions effortlessly, because for any fixed offset k, PE(pos+k) can be represented as a linear function of PE(pos). This property makes it easier for the self-attention mechanism to learn to attend based on relative distances between tokens, so the model doesn't need to learn separate rules for an offset of +2 at position 5 versus position 50.\n\n## Additional Properties\n\nThe sinusoidal functions vary smoothly with position, meaning that the positional encodings for adjacent positions are similar, reflecting that adjacent words often have closely related contextual roles, which contrasts with potentially abrupt changes in less structured encoding schemes.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"3e66b58c-f1fb-40d8-b5fe-bcd2cc44d2ac","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:40:22.006Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"b6e3c9d9-d028-4543-985e-d112bf760127","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Overview of Positional Encoding Methods\n\nFive distinct approaches to injecting positional information into transformers are: sinusoidal encoding, learned embeddings, relative position encoding, Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE), and Attention with Linear Biases (ALiBi).\n\n## How ALiBi Works\n\nALiBi negatively biases attention scores with a linearly decreasing penalty proportional to the distance between the relevant key and query, and eliminates position embeddings. ALiBi does not add positional embeddings to word embeddings; instead, it biases query-key attention scores with a penalty proportional to their distance, enabling a 1.3 billion parameter model trained on sequences of length 1024 to extrapolate to sequences of length 2048, achieving the same perplexity as a sinusoidal model trained on 2048 but training 11% faster and using 11% less memory.\n\n## Comparison with RoPE and Sinusoidal\n\nALiBi modifies attention scores by adding a head-specific linear distance penalty acting on token interactions, while RoPE modifies the query and key vectors by rotating them using sinusoidal functions, acting on token representation.\n\nALiBi's linear bias grows predictably with distance, making it naturally suited for extrapolation. In contrast, as input length increases beyond training length, sinusoidal models' performance degrades almost immediately while ALiBi's performance continues improving up to ~2-3x training length before plateauing.\n\n## Extrapolation Performance\n\nALiBi maintains the best extrapolation due to its simple linear bias design, outperforming both RoPE and sinusoidal approaches. Both ALiBi and RoPE have similar performance and are much better than APE (Absolute Positional Embeddings), with ALiBi having the distinct advantage of being faster to train.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"c0091b69-dcb1-44eb-a49f-df2f13c537f9","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:40:22.615Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"cb8c1e59-0fa8-4463-b58b-cdbc35ce9dd6","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

For a query vector **q** = (q₁, q₂, q₃, q₄, ..., q_d), it becomes: **q** = (q₁+iq₂, q₃+iq₄, ..., q_(d-1)+iq_d).\n\n## Key Mathematical Properties\n\nThe inner product between rotated query and key preserves relative position:\n\n```\n⟨RoPE(q_j, m), RoPE(k_j, n)⟩ = q_j·k_j·e^((m-n)i·ε)\n```\n\nThis demonstrates how \"absolute position changes disappear when both vectors rotate identically, preserving only their relative separation.\"\n\n## Matrix Implementation\n\nFor computational efficiency, the rotation becomes a block-diagonal matrix:\n\n```\nM_j = [cos(m·θ_j)  -sin(m·θ_j)]\n      [sin(m·θ_j)   cos(m·θ_j)]\n```\n\nApplied element-wise to pairs of dimensions.\n\n## Frequency Selection Strategy\n\nThe source specifies: **inv_freq = 1.0 / (10000^((arange(0, dim, 2)) / dim))**\n\nThis derives from the original transformer sinusoidal approach, where frequencies decrease exponentially across dimensions. The paper doesn't explicitly justify *why* these specific frequencies were chosen, only that they follow established conventions from prior positional encoding work.\n\n## Why This Design Works\n\nThe approach leverages \"geometric definitions of dot products\"—dot products depend on vector magnitudes and angles between them. By encoding position as pure rotation, relative angular differences remain invariant under simultaneous rotations, making attention operations preserve relative positional information naturally."}]},"uuid":"233c244f-6299-4539-9e72-047290c8f876","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:40:41.301Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"1cff78f0-fa29-4ea1-a465-00b6af204d4f","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}
{"parentUuid":"3ca4256c-1b11-410b-9f7d-05b180d51093","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"c790f000-3c73-4004-a5a7-bbe542fdebd4","agentId":"ae2d63ccfbc72ab8a","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_011U7Sfx1SJZHudPDvVb4Gfn","type":"tool_result","content":"# Mathematical Analysis of Positional Encoding\n\n## Desirable Properties (Pr_n)\n\nThe article establishes five key properties for an ideal positional encoding scheme:\n\n### **Property 1: Unique Encoding per Position**\nEach position needs a unique encoding consistent across sequence lengths. A token at position 5 should have identical encoding whether the sequence is length 10 or 10,000.\n\n### **Property 2: Linear Relation Between Encoded Positions**\nThe relationship between positions should be mathematically simple. Computing the encoding for position p+k from position p should be straightforward, enabling the model to learn positional patterns efficiently.\n\n### **Property 3: Generalization to Longer Sequences**\nThe encoding must adapt to sequences longer than those in training without violating other properties.\n\n### **Property 4: Deterministic, Learnable Process**\nEncodings should derive from a deterministic process that models can efficiently learn.\n\n### **Property 5: Extensibility to Multiple Dimensions**\nThe scheme must naturally extend from 1D to nD for multimodal data (images, volumetric data).\n\n---\n\n## Mathematical Foundation\n\n### **Sinusoidal Positional Encoding**\n\nThe foundational formula (Vaswani et al., 2017):\n\n$$PE_{(pos,2i)} = \\sin\\left(\\frac{pos}{10000^{2i/d}}\\right)$$\n\n$$PE_{(pos,2i+1)} = \\cos\\left(\\frac{pos}{10000^{2i/d}}\\right)$$\n\nWhere:\n- **pos** = token position index\n- **i** = component index in the positional encoding vector\n- **d** = model dimension\n- **θ = 10,000** = base wavelength\n\n**Key insight**: The base of 10,000 yields ~63,000 unique positions theoretically, derived from:\n$$2\\pi \\cdot 10000 \\approx 62,832 \\text{ unique positions}$$\n\n### **Linear Transformation via Rotation Matrix**\n\nThe critical mathematical discovery: sinusoidal encodings encode relative position through **rotation matrices**.\n\nFor a transformation matrix **M** that shifts position by k:\n\n$$\\mathbf{M} \\cdot \\begin{bmatrix} \\sin(\\omega_i p) \\\\ \\cos(\\omega_i p) \\end{bmatrix} = \\begin{bmatrix} \\sin(\\omega_i(p + k)) \\\\ \\cos(\\omega_i(p + k)) \\end{bmatrix}$$\n\nUsing trigonometric addition theorems, solving for the transformation coefficients yields:\n\n$$\\mathbf{M_k} = \\begin{bmatrix} \\cos(\\omega_i k) & \\sin(\\omega_i k) \\\\ -\\sin(\\omega_i k) & \\cos(\\omega_i k) \\end{bmatrix}$$\n\n**This is the 2D rotation matrix**, where $\\omega_i = \\frac{1}{10000^{2i/d}}$ represents geometric frequency progression.\n\n---\n\n## Rotary Positional Encoding (RoPE)\n\nBuilding on the rotation insight, RoPE applies rotations directly in the attention mechanism's query-key space rather than in embeddings.\n\n### **Core Formula**\n\nFor query/key vector **q** at position p, apply block-diagonal rotation matrix:\n\n$$R(\\mathbf{q}, p) = \\begin{pmatrix} \\mathbf{M_1} & & & \\\\ & \\mathbf{M_2} & & \\\\ & & \\ddots & \\\\ & & & \\mathbf{M_{d/2}} \\end{pmatrix} \\begin{pmatrix} q_1 \\\\ q_2 \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ q_d \\end{pmatrix}$$\n\n### **Efficient Implementation**\n\nRather than matrix multiplication, apply rotations to 2D element pairs:\n\n$$R_{\\Theta,p}^d q = \\begin{pmatrix} q_1 \\\\ q_2 \\\\ q_3 \\\\ q_4 \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ q_{d-1} \\\\ q_d \\end{pmatrix} \\otimes \\begin{pmatrix} \\cos p\\theta_1 \\\\ \\cos p\\theta_1 \\\\ \\cos p\\theta_2 \\\\ \\cos p\\theta_2 \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ \\cos p\\theta_{d/2} \\\\ \\cos p\\theta_{d/2} \\end{pmatrix} + \\begin{pmatrix} -q_2 \\\\ q_1 \\\\ -q_4 \\\\ q_3 \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ -q_d \\\\ q_{d-1} \\end{pmatrix} \\otimes \\begin{pmatrix} \\sin p\\theta_1 \\\\ \\sin p\\theta_1 \\\\ \\sin p\\theta_2 \\\\ \\sin p\\theta_2 \\\\ \\vdots \\\\ \\sin p\\theta_{d/2} \\\\ \\sin p\\theta_{d/2} \\end{pmatrix}$$\n\n### **Geometric Interpretation**\n\nThe dot product relation explains why rotation works:\n\n$$\\vec{a} \\cdot \\vec{b} = |\\vec{a}| |\\vec{b}| \\cos \\theta$$\n\nRotating vectors modulates attention scores purely through angle changes without affecting semantic norm preservation.\n\n---\n\n## Frequency Selection Strategy Comparison\n\n| Approach | Formula | Issues | Advantages |\n|----------|---------|--------|------------|\n| **Integer** | `pos` | Magnitude exceeds embeddings; violates Property 1 | Direct position representation |\n| **Normalized Integer** | `pos/N` | Sequence-dependent; violates Property 1 | Bounded values |\n| **Binary** | Bit decomposition | Discrete, jumpy gradients | Unique, consistent encoding |\n| **Sinusoidal** | $\\sin/\\cos(pos/10000^{2i/d})$ | Additive pollution of embeddings | Smooth, learnable, satisfies Pr₁-Pr₄ |\n| **RoPE** | Multiplicative rotation | Complex implementation | Multiplicative (preserves norm), Satisfies all Pr₁-Pr₅, relative encoding |\n\n---\n\n## Multi-Dimensional Extension\n\nFor nD data, apply rotations independently per dimension:\n\n$$R^{2D}(\\mathbf{q}, m, i) = \\text{Rotate}(\\mathbf{q}, m \\cdot \\theta_k) \\text{ for x-offset}$$\n$$R^{2D}(\\mathbf{q}, i, j) = \\text{Rotate}(\\mathbf{q}, i \\cdot \\theta_k) \\text{ for y-offset}$$\n\n**Critical insight**: Pair components **within the same dimension**, not arbitrarily, to prevent intermixing of spatial offsets.\n\n---\n\n## Self-Attention Context\n\nThe motivation for RoPE's design addresses a fundamental limitation:\n\n$$\\text{Attn}(Q, K, V) = \\text{softmax}\\left(\\frac{QK^T}{\\sqrt{d_k}}\\right)V$$\n\nTraditional additive positional encoding pollutes semantic information. The lost-in-the-middle effect is a well-documented phenomenon where LLMs perform significantly worse when relevant information sits in the middle of their context rather than at the beginning or end.\n\n## The U-Shaped Attention Curve\n\nWhen you plot accuracy against document position, the curve forms a U shape. High at the edges, low in the middle. Tokens at the beginning and end of the context receive disproportionately strong attention. Tokens in the middle receive less.\n\n## Primacy and Recency Bias\n\nHumans remember items at the beginning of a list (primacy) and the end (recency) better than items in the middle. LLMs exhibit the same bias, but for architectural rather than cognitive reasons.\n\n## Positional Encoding as the Root Cause\n\nThe U-shaped attention curve is caused by positional encoding biases in the transformer architecture. Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE), used in most modern LLMs, introduces a decay effect that makes models attend more strongly to tokens at the beginning and end of sequences.\n\n## Proposed Fixes\n\n\"Found in the Middle\" (UW/MIT/Google, ACL 2024) proposed a calibration method to counteract this bias. The standard RoPE encodes positions as:\n\n**f(x, m) = x·e^(im·θ)**\n\nMs-PoE transforms this to:\n\n**f(x, m/r) = x·e^(i(m/r)·θ)**\n\nThis compression \"forces long-distance tokens to reside in the shorter range,\" mitigating RoPE's inherent long-term decay effect that contributes to the lost-in-the-middle problem.\n\n## Position-Awareness Scoring\n\nRather than applying uniform scaling across all attention heads, Ms-PoE computes a Position-Awareness Score for each head:\n\n**𝒮_P = (1/l) Σ(A_i ≥ α·(1/l)ΣA_i)**\n\nHere, *A* represents attention scores for the most recent query, *l* is sequence length, and *α* (default=3) is a threshold hyperparameter. Heads with higher 𝒮_P scores demonstrate positional awareness—they capture relevant information across position shifts.\n\n## Per-Head Scaling Ratios\n\nMs-PoE assigns linearly-distributed scaling ratios from 1.2 to 1.8 across heads, sorted by position-awareness score:\n\n**r_i = 1.2 + (i−1)(1.8−1.2)/(n_h−1)**\n\nPosition-aware heads receive ratios near 1.0 (preserving pre-training knowledge), while position-insensitive heads receive larger ratios (1.2–1.8), creating multi-scale position encoding that \"forms a multi-scale context fusion.\""}]},"uuid":"3cde55e1-6f5d-4c51-9167-0b570e2c3bcd","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:41:33.474Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"971d73af-4165-49df-89f6-cbd6970cb7da","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

search results, here's what I found about ALiBi (Attention with Linear Biases) and its slope formula:\n\n## ALiBi Formula and Head-Specific Slopes\n\nALiBi modifies the standard attention mechanism by adding a static, non-learned bias after the query-key dot product, using the formula: softmax(q_i K^T + m · [−(i −1), ..., −2, −1, 0]), where scalar m is a head-specific slope fixed before training.\n\n## Geometric Sequence Formula: 2^(-8/n)\n\nTo decide the value of 'm' for a particular head, the authors use a simple geometric progression which has both the start and the ratio as 2^(-8/n_heads), where n is the number of attention heads.\n\nThe heads' slopes are a geometric sequence that starts at 2^(-alibi_bias_max/num_heads) and uses that same value as its ratio.\n\n## Concrete Example with 8 Heads\n\nFor instance when the number of heads is 8 the slopes are 2^(-1/1), 2^(-1/2), …, 2^(-1/8). Or more intuitively, if we have 8 heads, the slopes would be the geometric sequence: 1/2^1, 1/2^2, …, 1/2^8.\n\n## Why Head-Specific Penalties?\n\nALiBi has an inductive bias towards recency; it penalizes attention scores between distant query-key pairs, with the penalty increasing as the distance between a key and a query grows. The different heads increase their penalties at different rates, depending on the slope magnitude.\n\nThis design creates a spectrum from syntax-focused (local) to semantics-focused (global) heads.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"08545bfd-575e-4b92-822e-603ae8e37f72","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:41:47.567Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"097ae439-9708-4f6e-aa76-5054eefcec9e","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Main Results\n\nMontgomery's pair correlation conjecture is a conjecture made by Hugh Montgomery (1973) that the pair correlation between pairs of zeros of the Riemann zeta function (normalized to have unit average spacing) is the same as the pair correlation function of random Hermitian matrices.\n\nFreeman Dyson pointed out that the Gaussian unitary ensemble (GUE) has the same pair correlation function.\n\n## Empirical Verification\n\nIn the 1980s, Odlyzko confirmed the distribution of the spacings between non-trivial zeros using detailed numerical calculations and demonstrated that the distribution would agree with the distribution of spacings of GUE random matrix eigenvalues using Cray X-MP.\n\nThis study tests the Montgomery pair correlation conjecture as well as some further conjectures that predict that the zeros of the zeta function behave like eigenvalues of random Hermitian matrices. The agreement between actual statistics for zeros of the zeta function and conjectured results is generally good, and improves at larger heights.\n\n## Connection to Prime Distribution\n\nGoldston and Montgomery found an equivalence under the Riemann Hypothesis between the pair correlation of zeros and the variance for the number of primes in short intervals.\n\nThe search results provide comprehensive information on this deep and fascinating connection between number theory and random matrix theory.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"71917df3-8d5b-44d1-8068-07d4724d3ee2","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:41:48.606Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"83e9931d-5ad4-4ae6-a6aa-6e19f112dcb4","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

Positional encoding is a crucial\ncomponent to allow attention-based deep model architectures such as Transformer\nto address sequences or images where the position of information matters. In\nthis paper, we propose a novel positional encoding method based on learnable\nFourier features. Instead of hard-coding each position as a token or a vector,\nwe represent each position, which can be multi-dimensional, as a trainable\nencoding based on learnable Fourier feature mapping, modulated with a\nmulti-layer perceptron. The representation is particularly advantageous for a\nspatial multi-dimensional position, e.g., pixel positions on an image, where\nL_2 distances or more complex positional relationships need to be captured.\nOur experiments based on several public benchmark tasks show that our learnable\nFourier feature representation for multi-dimensional positional encoding\noutperforms existing methods by both improving the accuracy and allowing faster\nconvergence.\n\n**AI Keywords**: attentional mechanisms, positional encoding, Transformer, learnable Fourier features, multi-layer perceptron, $L_2$ distances, spatial multi-dimensional position, pixel positions, accuracy, convergence\nUpvoted 1 times. Many works\nfocus on approximating the dot-then-exponentiate softmax function in the\noriginal attention, leading to sub-quadratic or even linear-complexity\nTransformer architectures. However, we show that these methods cannot be\napplied to more powerful attention modules that go beyond the\ndot-then-exponentiate style, e.g., Transformers with relative positional\nencoding (RPE). Since in many state-of-the-art models, relative positional\nencoding is used as default, designing efficient Transformers that can\nincorporate RPE is appealing. In this paper, we propose a novel way to\naccelerate attention calculation for Transformers with RPE on top of the\nkernelized attention. Based upon the observation that relative positional\nencoding forms a Toeplitz matrix, we mathematically show that kernelized\nattention with RPE can be calculated efficiently using Fast Fourier Transform\n(FFT). With FFT, our method achieves O(nlog n) time complexity.\nInterestingly, we further demonstrate that properly using relative positional\nencoding can mitigate the training instability problem of vanilla kernelized\nattention. On a wide range of tasks, we empirically show that our models can be\ntrained from scratch without any optimization issues. The learned model\nperforms better than many efficient Transformer variants and is faster than\nstandard Transformer in the long-sequence regime.\n\n**AI Keywords**: attention module, Transformer, dot-then-exponentiate softmax, linear-complexity Transformer, relative positional encoding, RPE, kernelized attention, Toeplitz matrix, Fast Fourier Transform, FFT, nlogn time complexity, training instability, efficient Transformer variants\n**Link to paper:** [https://hf.co/papers/2106.12566](https://hf.co/papers/2106.12566)\n\n---\n\n## Position Coupling: Improving Length Generalization of Arithmetic\n  Transformers Using Task Structure\n\nPublished on Published on 31 May, 2024\n**Authors:** Hanseul Cho, Jaeyoung Cha, Pranjal Awasthi, Srinadh Bhojanapalli, Anupam Gupta, Chulhee Yun\n\n### Abstract\n\nEven for simple arithmetic tasks like integer addition, it is challenging for\nTransformers to generalize to longer sequences than those encountered during\ntraining. To tackle this problem, we propose position coupling, a simple yet\neffective method that directly embeds the structure of the tasks into the\npositional encoding of a (decoder-only) Transformer. Taking a departure from\nthe vanilla absolute position mechanism assigning unique position IDs to each\nof the tokens, we assign the same position IDs to two or more \"relevant\"\ntokens; for integer addition tasks, we regard digits of the same significance\nas in the same position. On the empirical side, we show that with the proposed\nposition coupling, our models trained on 1 to 30-digit additions can generalize\nup to 200-digit additions (6.67x of the trained length). On the theoretical\nside, we prove that a 1-layer Transformer with coupled positions can solve the\naddition task involving exponentially many digits, whereas any 1-layer\nTransformer without positional information cannot entirely solve it. We also\ndemonstrate that position coupling can be applied to other algorithmic tasks\nsuch as Nx2 multiplication and a two-dimensional task.\n\n**AI Keywords**: Transformers, position coupling, positional encoding, integer addition, digit significance, Nx2 multiplication, two-dimensional task\n**Link to paper:** [https://hf.co/papers/2405.20671](https://hf.co/papers/2405.20671)\n\n---\n\n## Context-aware Rotary Position Embedding\n\nPublished on Published on 30 Jul, 2025\n**Authors:** Ali Veisi, Delaram Fartoot, Hamidreza Amirzadeh\n\n### Abstract\n\nPositional encoding is a vital component of Transformer architectures,\nenabling models to incorporate sequence order into self-attention mechanisms.\nRotary Positional Embeddings (RoPE) have become a widely adopted solution due\nto their compatibility with relative position encoding and computational\nefficiency. However, RoPE relies on static, input-independent sinusoidal\nfrequency patterns, limiting its ability to model context-sensitive\nrelationships. In this work, we propose CARoPE (Context-Aware Rotary Positional\nEmbedding), a novel generalization of RoPE that dynamically generates\nhead-specific frequency patterns conditioned on token embeddings. This design\nintroduces token- and context-sensitive positional representations while\npreserving RoPE efficiency and architectural simplicity. CARoPE computes\ninput-dependent phase shifts using a bounded transformation of token embeddings\nand integrates them into the rotary mechanism across attention heads. We\nevaluate CARoPE on the FineWeb-Edu-10B dataset using GPT-2 variants trained on\nnext-token prediction tasks. Experimental results show that CARoPE consistently\noutperforms RoPE and other common positional encoding baselines, achieving\nsignificantly lower perplexity, even at longer context lengths. Additionally,\nCARoPE enables faster training throughput without sacrificing model stability.\nThese findings demonstrate that CARoPE offers a scalable, expressive, and\nefficient upgrade to existing positional encoding strategies in Transformer\nmodels.\n\n**AI Keywords**: Positional encoding, Transformer architectures, self-attention mechanisms, Rotary Positional Embeddings, RoPE, relative position encoding, computational efficiency, CARoPE, Context-Aware Rotary Positional Embedding, token embeddings, attention heads, FineWeb-Edu-10B dataset, GPT-2, next-token prediction tasks, perplexity, training throughput, model stability\n**Link to paper:** [https://hf.co/papers/2507.23083](https://hf.co/papers/2507.23083)\n\n---\n\n## Rotary Positional Embeddings as Phase Modulation: Theoretical Bounds on the RoPE Base for Long-Context Transformers\n\nPublished on Published on 11 Feb, 2026\n**Authors:** Feilong Liu\n\n### Abstract\n\nRotary positional embeddings (RoPE) are widely used in large language models to encode token positions through multiplicative rotations, yet their behavior at long context lengths remains poorly characterized. In this work, we reinterpret RoPE as phase modulation applied to a bank of complex oscillators, enabling analysis through classical signal processing theory.\n  Under this formulation, we derive principled lower bounds on the RoPE base parameter that are necessary to preserve positional coherence over a target context length. These include a fundamental aliasing bound, analogous to a Nyquist limit, and a DC-component stability bound that constrains phase drift in low-frequency positional modes. We further extend this analysis to deep transformers, showing that repeated rotary modulation across layers compounds angular misalignment, tightening the base requirement as depth increases.\n  Complementing these results, we derive a precision-dependent upper bound on the RoPE base arising from finite floating-point resolution. Beyond this limit, incremental phase updates become numerically indistinguishable, leading to positional erasure even in the absence of aliasing. Together, the lower and upper bounds define a precision- and depth-dependent feasibility region a Goldilocks zone for long-context transformers.\n  We validate the framework through a comprehensive case study of state-of-the-art models, including LLaMA, Mistral, and DeepSeek variants, showing that observed successes, failures, and community retrofits align closely with the predicted bounds. Notably, models that violate the stability bound exhibit attention collapse and long-range degradation, while attempts to scale beyond one million tokens encounter a hard precision wall independent of architecture or training.\n\n**AI Keywords**: rotary positional embeddings, phase modulation, complex oscillators, signal processing theory, RoPE base parameter, positional coherence, aliasing bound, Nyquist limit, DC-component stability bound, phase drift, deep transformers, angular misalignment, floating-point resolution, positional erasure, attention collapse, long-range degradation, Goldilocks zone\n**Link to paper:** [https://hf.co/papers/2602.10959](https://hf.co/papers/2602.10959)\n\n---\n\n## Conditional Positional Encodings for Vision Transformers\n\nPublished on Published on 22 Feb, 2021\n**Authors:** Xiangxiang Chu, Zhi Tian, Bo Zhang, Xinlong Wang ([xinlongwang](https://hf.co/xinlongwang)), Chunhua Shen\n\n### Abstract\n\nWe propose a conditional positional encoding (CPE) scheme for vision\nTransformers. Unlike previous fixed or learnable positional encodings, which\nare pre-defined and independent of input tokens, CPE is dynamically generated\nand conditioned on the local neighborhood of the input tokens. As a result, CPE\ncan easily generalize to the input sequences that are longer than what the\nmodel has ever seen during training. Besides, CPE can keep the desired\ntranslation-invariance in the image classification task, resulting in improved\nperformance. Hitherto, the rationale for the effectiveness of\nthese positional encodings has been solely studied through a Fourier lens. In\nthis paper, we strive to broaden this understanding by showing that alternative\nnon-Fourier embedding functions can indeed be used for positional encoding.\nMoreover, we show that their performance is entirely determined by a trade-off\nbetween the stable rank of the embedded matrix and the distance preservation\nbetween embedded coordinates. Here's what the paper \"Rethinking Positional Encoding\" is about:\n\n## Overview\n\nThe paper broadens understanding beyond studying positional encodings through a Fourier lens by showing that alternative non-Fourier embedding functions can indeed be used for positional encoding. \n\n## Key Findings\n\nPerformance is entirely determined by a trade-off between the stable rank of the embedded matrix and the distance preservation between embedded coordinates. The ubiquitous Fourier feature mapping of position is a special case that fulfills these conditions.\n\n## Theoretical Framework\n\nThe paper presents a more general theory to analyze positional encoding in terms of shifted basis functions. Positional encoding can be accomplished via systematic sampling of shifted continuous basis functions, where the shifts are determined by the coordinate positions.\n\n## Two Key Factors\n\nThe effectiveness of the proposed embedding scheme primarily relies on two factors: (i) the approximate matrix rank of the embedded representation across positions, and (ii) the distance preservation between the embedded coordinates. A higher approximate matrix rank causes better memorization of the training data, while the distance preservation correlates with generalization.\n\n## Practical Example\n\nThe paper employs a Gaussian signal as the embedding function and shows that it can deliver on-par performance with Fourier frequency mapping while being more efficient in terms of embedding dimension and less volatile.\n\nThe paper was published in July 2021 (arXiv:2107.02561) by Jianqiao Zheng, Sameera Ramasinghe, and Simon Lucey.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"8ac30fc7-16ad-4bd4-99c2-f3378f7af61d","timestamp":"2026-03-27T06:43:03.469Z","sourceToolAssistantUUID":"dedebac7-4588-4e70-9a72-232507977360","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"C:\\Files\\Models\\CosySim","sessionId":"8cd99b00-f117-4df7-b6fd-757d36dd8a99","version":"2.1.84","gitBranch":"master","slug":"dazzling-wiggling-quiche"}

{"parentUuid":"ed6aafbc-c077-46a6-a8e5-4bd3064dd04e","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"ae2d63ccfbc72ab8a","message":{"model":"claude-opus-4-6","id":"msg_01SrZgufF2ezLHq7EW3pXwnT","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_015vGhzqHLkP6gYxLsxt9YFg","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"C:\\Users\\Knack\\.claude\\plans\\dazzling-wiggling-quiche-agent-ae2d63ccfbc72ab8a.md","content":"# Research Summary: Prime Numbers, Riemann Zeta Function, and Transformer Positional Encodings\n\n## Comprehensive Research Report — 2026-03-27\n\n---\n\n## 1. Existing Work on Prime-Based Positional Encodings\n\n**Finding: No published work exists using prime-number-based frequency selection for transformer positional encodings.** This is a genuinely novel research direction.\n\nThe closest related work falls into several categories:\n\n- **CoPE (Complex Positional Encoding)** (Amballa, Aug 2025, arXiv:2508.18308) — Uses complex-valued embeddings where the real part encodes semantics and the imaginary part encodes position. Introduces \"phase-aware attention\" in the first transformer layer. Does not exhibit long-term decay. Outperforms RoPE, sinusoidal, and learned encodings on GLUE.\n\n- **CARoPE (Context-Aware Rotary Positional Embedding)** (Veisi et al., Jul 2025, arXiv:2507.23083) — Dynamically generates head-specific frequency patterns conditioned on token embeddings, replacing RoPE's static sinusoidal frequencies.\n\n- **DoPE (Denoising Positional Encoding)** (Xiong et al., Nov 2025, arXiv:2511.09146) — Reinterprets attention with positional encoding as a noisy feature map. Uses truncated matrix entropy to detect outlier frequency bands. 98 upvotes on HuggingFace.\n\n- **\"Rethinking Positional Encoding\"** (Zheng et al., 2021, arXiv:2107.02561) — **Critical theoretical result**: shows that Fourier-based frequency mapping is just one special case of a broader class. Performance depends on two factors: (1) **stable rank** of the embedded matrix, and (2) **distance preservation** between embedded coordinates. Non-Fourier bases (e.g., Gaussians) can match Fourier performance. This paper provides the theoretical justification for why alternative frequency bases (including zeta-zero-based) could work.\n\n- **RoPE as Phase Modulation** (Liu, Feb 2026, arXiv:2602.10959) — Reinterprets RoPE as phase modulation on complex oscillators. Derives Nyquist-like aliasing bounds and DC-stability bounds on the RoPE base parameter. Defines a \"Goldilocks zone\" for the base parameter. This framework could directly inform analysis of zeta-zero frequencies.\n\n- **Learnable Fourier Features** (Li et al., 2021, arXiv:2106.02795) — Trainable Fourier feature mapping for multi-dimensional positions.\n\n**Key Insight**: The field is actively exploring alternatives to geometric frequency progressions, but nobody has explored number-theoretic frequency bases.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Complex-Valued Attention and Complex-Valued Neural Networks\n\n### ComplexFormer (May 2025, arXiv:2505.10222) — WITHDRAWN\n- Introduced Complex Multi-Head Attention (CMHA)\n- Each head independently models semantic and positional differences unified in the complex plane\n- Per-Head Euler Transformation converts real-valued Q/K projections into polar-form complex vectors\n- Adaptive Differential Rotation: `exp[i(Adapt(A_smn,i) + Delta(P_mn,i))]`\n- **Paper withdrawn** — \"underlying experiment is currently incomplete\"\n\n### Building Blocks for Complex-Valued Transformer (ICASSP 2023, arXiv:2306.09827)\n- Multiple versions of complex-valued Scaled Dot-Product Attention\n- Complex-valued layer normalization\n- Enables deep learning directly on complex-valued signals without projection to R^2\n\n### Holographic Transformer (Oct 2025, arXiv:2509.19331)\n- Physics-inspired architecture incorporating wave interference into self-attention\n- Each value is rotated by its phase offset before summation\n- Models constructive and destructive interference\n- Attention weights are invariant under global phase rotation\n- Output transforms covariantly\n\n### Complex-Valued Neural Networks: The Liouville Constraint\nKey survey: arXiv:2407.19258 (Jul 2024)\n\n**Liouville's Theorem** creates a fundamental tension: no function can be simultaneously bounded AND holomorphic (differentiable everywhere) over the entire complex plane. This constrains activation function design:\n- **Fully complex activation functions**: attempt to balance boundedness and differentiability\n- **Split activation functions**: apply separate operations to real and imaginary parts (practical but sacrifice analyticity)\n- **Complex amplitude-phase activation functions**: newer approach\n\nThree backpropagation approaches for CVNNs:\n1. Complex derivative approach\n2. Partial derivatives approach\n3. Algorithms incorporating the Cauchy-Riemann equations\n\n**Applications where CVNNs excel**: signal processing, speech enhancement, wireless communications, radar — anywhere data is naturally complex-valued.\n\n---\n\n## 3. RoPE: How It Uses Complex Numbers and Frequency Selection\n\n### Core Mathematical Framework\n\nRoPE treats pairs of embedding dimensions as complex numbers. For query vector `q = (q1, q2, q3, q4, ..., qd)`, it becomes:\n```\nq = (q1 + iq2, q3 + iq4, ..., q_{d-1} + iq_d) in C^{d/2}\n```\n\nThe rotation for position m at dimension j:\n```\nf(q_j, m) = q_j * e^{i*m*theta_j}\n```\n\n### The Frequency Formula\n```\ntheta_j = base^{-2j/d}    where base = 10000\n```\n\nEquivalently:\n```\ninv_freq = 1.0 / (10000^(arange(0, dim, 2) / dim))\n```\n\nThis creates wavelengths in a **geometric progression from 2*pi to 10000*2*pi**.\n\n### Why Base 10000?\n**There is NO mathematical justification.** The EleutherAI YaRN blog states: the paper provides no derivation. The choice is purely empirical. In fact, Liu (2026) found that 10000 is actually the *worst* base value for RoPE-based extrapolation in the fine-tuning phase.\n\n### Rotation Matrix Implementation\nFor each dimension pair, apply:\n```\nM_j = [cos(m*theta_j)  -sin(m*theta_j)]\n      [sin(m*theta_j)   cos(m*theta_j)]\n```\n\n### Key Property: Relative Position Preservation\n```\n<RoPE(q_j, m), RoPE(k_j, n)> = q_j * k_j * e^{(m-n)*i*theta_j}\n```\nThe dot product depends only on the relative position (m-n), not absolute positions.\n\n### Efficient Implementation (element-wise, no matrix multiply)\n```\nRoPE(q, p) = q * cos(p*theta) + rotate_half(q) * sin(p*theta)\n```\n\n### Extensions and Scaling Methods\n- **NTK-Aware Scaling**: `b' = b * s^{|D|/(|D|-2)}` — spreads interpolation pressure across dimensions\n- **YaRN**: Piecewise frequency scaling with ramp function: `h(theta_d) = (1-gamma)*(theta_d/s) + gamma*theta_d`\n- **CARoPE**: Content-dependent frequencies per head\n\n---\n\n## 4. \"Lost in the Middle\" — The U-Shaped Attention Problem\n\n### The Core Paper\n**\"Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts\"** (Liu et al., Jul 2023, arXiv:2307.03172)\nPublished in TACL 2024. Stanford/UW/Google.\n\n### Key Findings\n- Performance is **highest** when relevant information appears at the **beginning or end** of the context\n- Performance **degrades by 30%+** when information is in the **middle**\n- This holds for ALL tested models, including those explicitly trained for long contexts\n- The curve is **U-shaped**: high accuracy at edges, low in the middle\n- Tested on multi-document QA and key-value retrieval tasks\n\n### Root Cause: Positional Encoding Bias\n- RoPE introduces a **long-term decay effect** that makes models attend more strongly to tokens at the beginning and end\n- This mimics human **primacy and recency bias** but for architectural reasons\n- The attention distribution is not uniform — it's biased by position\n\n### \"Lost in the Middle at Birth\" (Mar 2026, arXiv:2603.10123)\n- Shows the U-shaped bias is an **inherent property** of the attention mechanism, not just a training artifact\n\n### Proposed Solutions\n\n**Ms-PoE (Multi-scale Positional Encoding)** (Zhang et al., NeurIPS 2024, arXiv:2403.04797):\n- Plug-and-play approach, no fine-tuning needed\n- Rescales position indices: `f(x, m/r) = x * e^{i*(m/r)*theta}` — compresses long-distance tokens into shorter range\n- Assigns **different scaling ratios per attention head** based on Position-Awareness Score\n- Scaling ratios: linearly distributed from 1.2 to 1.8 across heads\n- Position-Awareness Score: `S_P = (1/l) * sum(A_i >= alpha * (1/l) * sum(A_i))` where alpha=3\n- **Result**: +3.8 average accuracy on Zero-SCROLLS benchmark\n\n**This is directly relevant**: A zeta-zero-based frequency selection could potentially create more uniform attention across positions by avoiding the systematic decay inherent in geometric progressions.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Number Theory and Neural Network Architecture\n\n### Direct Applications\n- **ZetaNet**: Deep neural network using convolutional architecture with complex-valued activation functions to approximate the zeta function and locate its zeros\n- **Neural prediction of zeta zeros**: Reformulated as time-series prediction (Cadenas et al., various papers)\n- **Prime classification**: Sparse encoding with conventional neural nets achieves 99%+ recall on primes (arXiv:2402.03363)\n\n### The Quasicrystal Connection (THE KEY INSIGHT)\n**\"Quasicrystal Scattering and the Riemann Zeta Function\"** (Oct 2024, arXiv:2410.03673)\n\nThis paper establishes the deep mathematical connection:\n\n1. **Primes as a quasicrystal**: Place scatterers at positions `chi_n = ln(p_n)` (logarithms of primes). This creates approximately constant density.\n\n2. **Fourier transform encodes zeta function**:\n   ```\n   chi_hat_L(k) = sum p_n^{-2*pi*i*k}\n   ```\n   Setting `s = 2*pi*i*k`, this becomes the prime sum `P_L(s) = sum p_n^{-s}`, connected to:\n   ```\n   -zeta'(s)/zeta(s) = sum Lambda(n) * n^{-s}\n   ```\n\n3. **Zeros appear as spectral peaks**: Non-trivial zeros of zeta(s) appear as poles of the logarithmic derivative, producing resonant peaks at positions `gamma_m / (2*pi)`.\n\n4. **Fourier self-duality implies RH**: The normalized scattering amplitude:\n   ```\n   chi_tilde(k) = 1 - sum_rho (x^{beta_m - 1/2} * e^{i*gamma_m*ln(x)} / rho_m) * delta(k - gamma_m/(2*pi))\n   ```\n   Self-duality requires `beta_m = 1/2` for all zeros.\n\n**Why this matters for positional encoding**: The zeta zeros are the \"natural frequencies\" of the prime number distribution. They form a quasicrystal — structured but non-periodic. Using them as frequency bases would create a positional encoding with quasi-random properties fundamentally different from geometric progressions.\n\n### Montgomery-Dyson Connection (Random Matrix Theory)\n- **Montgomery's Pair Correlation Conjecture** (1973): The pair correlation of normalized zeta zeros follows `1 - (sin(pi*u)/(pi*u))^2`\n- This is **identical** to the pair correlation of eigenvalues of GUE (Gaussian Unitary Ensemble) random matrices\n- Confirmed numerically by Odlyzko (1987) using Cray X-MP\n- **Implication**: Zeta zeros have the same statistical structure as eigenvalues of random Hermitian matrices — they exhibit **level repulsion** (zeros don't cluster) while being irregularly spaced\n\n---\n\n## 6. The First 20 Riemann Zeta Zeros (Imaginary Parts)\n\nAll nontrivial zeros have the form `1/2 + i*t`. The first 20 values of t (computed by Andrew Odlyzko, verified to 1000+ decimal places):\n\n| # | Imaginary Part (t) | Ratio t_n/t_1 | Gap to next |\n|---|-------------------|---------------|-------------|\n| 1 | 14.1347251417347 | 1.000 | 6.887 |\n| 2 | 21.0220396387716 | 1.487 | 3.989 |\n| 3 | 25.0108575801457 | 1.769 | 5.414 |\n| 4 | 30.4248761258595 | 2.153 | 2.510 |\n| 5 | 32.9350615877392 | 2.330 | 4.651 |\n| 6 | 37.5861781588257 | 2.659 | 3.333 |\n| 7 | 40.9187190121475 | 2.895 | 2.408 |\n| 8 | 43.3270732809150 | 3.065 | 4.678 |\n| 9 | 48.0051508811672 | 3.396 | 1.769 |\n| 10 | 49.7738324776723 | 3.521 | 3.196 |\n| 11 | 52.9703214777145 | 3.748 | 3.476 |\n| 12 | 56.4462476970634 | 3.994 | 2.901 |\n| 13 | 59.3470440026024 | 4.199 | 1.485 |\n| 14 | 60.8317785246098 | 4.304 | 4.281 |\n| 15 | 65.1125440480816 | 4.607 | 1.967 |\n| 16 | 67.0798105294942 | 4.746 | 2.467 |\n| 17 | 69.5464017111740 | 4.921 | 2.521 |\n| 18 | 72.0671576744819 | 5.099 | 3.638 |\n| 19 | 75.7046906990839 | 5.356 | 1.440 |\n| 20 | 77.1448400688748 | 5.458 | — |\n\n### Mathematical Properties Relevant to Positional Encoding\n\n1. **Irregular spacing (level repulsion)**: Gaps vary from 1.440 to 6.887 — NOT geometric, NOT arithmetic. The GUE pair correlation `1 - (sin(pi*u)/(pi*u))^2` means zeros repel each other (small gaps are rare).\n\n2. **Average spacing**: For zeros near height T, the average spacing is `2*pi / ln(T/(2*pi))`. As height increases, spacing decreases logarithmically.\n\n3. **Ratios are irrational and mutually incommensurable**: The ratios t_n/t_m are believed to be irrational for all n != m. This means the frequencies `omega_k = t_k` would produce rotations that **never exactly repeat** — unlike geometric progressions where `omega_k = base^{-2k/d}` creates systematic harmonic relationships.\n\n4. **Quasicrystal property**: The zeros form a quasicrystal (discrete support, discrete Fourier transform). This means they are structured but non-periodic — the ideal balance for positional encoding.\n\n5. **Self-similarity across scales**: The statistical distribution of zeros at any height looks the same (after normalization), following GUE statistics. This provides multi-scale coverage.\n\n---\n\n## 7. Original Sinusoidal Positional Encoding — Exact Formulas\n\nFrom **\"Attention Is All You Need\"** (Vaswani et al., NeurIPS 2017):\n\n### The Formula\n```\nPE(pos, 2i)   = sin(pos / 10000^{2i/d_model})\nPE(pos, 2i+1) = cos(pos / 10000^{2i/d_model})\n```\n\nWhere:\n- `pos` = position in the sequence (0, 1, 2, ...)\n- `i` = dimension index (0, 1, ..., d/2 - 1)\n- `d_model` = model dimension (typically 512 or 768)\n\n### Frequency Progression\nThe angular frequency for dimension pair i:\n```\nomega_i = 1 / 10000^{2i/d_model}\n```\n\nThe wavelength (period) for dimension pair i:\n```\nlambda_i = 2*pi * 10000^{2i/d_model}\n```\n\n**Geometric progression**: wavelengths range from `2*pi` (≈6.28 tokens, dimension 0) to `10000*2*pi` (≈62,832 tokens, dimension d/2-1).\n\n### Key Property: Relative Position as Linear Transformation\nFor any fixed offset phi, there exists a rotation matrix M:\n```\nM_{phi,k} = [cos(omega_k*phi)   sin(omega_k*phi)]\n            [-sin(omega_k*phi)  cos(omega_k*phi)]\n```\nSuch that:\n```\nM * [sin(omega_k*t), cos(omega_k*t)]^T = [sin(omega_k*(t+phi)), cos(omega_k*(t+phi))]^T\n```\n\nThis means PE(pos+k) is a linear function of PE(pos) — the model can learn to attend by relative positions.\n\n### Maximum Unique Positions\n```\n2*pi * 10000 ≈ 62,832 unique positions\n```\n\n### Why Geometric Progression?\nThe paper states sinusoidal was chosen because \"it may allow the model to extrapolate to sequence lengths longer than the ones encountered during training.\" The geometric progression gives:\n- **Low dimensions**: high frequencies, sensitive to nearby positions\n- **High dimensions**: low frequencies, sensitive to distant positions\n- Together: multi-scale coverage of the position space\n\n---\n\n## 8. Multi-Scale and Fractal Attention Patterns\n\n### Multi-Scale Positional Encoding (Ms-PoE)\nSee Section 4 above. The key innovation: different attention heads get different position scaling ratios, creating multi-scale context fusion.\n\n### Hierarchical Attention Architectures\n- **FasterViT** (ICLR 2024): Interleaved local + hierarchical attention blocks for short and long-range dependencies\n- **H-MHSA**: Hierarchical multi-head self-attention computing at multiple resolution levels\n- **MERIT**: Multi-scale backbone computing self-attention at multiple scales\n\n### GraphFractalNet (2024/2025, OpenReview)\n- Fractal attention mechanism constraining attention to recursively clustered subgraphs\n- Sub-quadratic complexity while retaining expressive power\n- Integrates spectral embeddings with dynamic graph rewiring\n\n### Multi-Scale Visualization of Attention (Vig, ACL 2019, arXiv:1906.05714)\n- Reveals that attention naturally operates at multiple scales\n- Different heads specialize in different scale patterns\n- Model view, head view, and neuron view for understanding\n\n### Progressive Implicit Networks (PINs, 2022, arXiv:2202.04713)\n- Exposes hierarchical MLP to **incremental sets of frequency encodings**\n- Learns scene representation at progressive levels of detail\n- Addresses the frequency spectrum challenge: high frequencies = noise in smooth areas, low frequencies = poor detail fitting\n\n**Key Insight for Zeta-Zero Encoding**: The irregular spacing of zeta zeros naturally creates a multi-scale frequency basis without explicit design. The gaps between zeros (1.44 to 6.89 for first 20) mean some dimension pairs capture fine positional differences while others capture coarse ones — but NOT in a simple geometric hierarchy.\n\n---\n\n## 9. ALiBi (Attention with Linear Biases)\n\n### Paper\n**\"Train Short, Test Long: Attention with Linear Biases Enables Input Length Extrapolation\"** (Press et al., ICLR 2022, arXiv:2108.12409)\n\n### Core Mechanism\nALiBi does NOT add positional embeddings. Instead, it adds a **static bias** to attention scores:\n```\na_i = softmax(q_i * K^T + m * [-(i-1), ..., -2, -1, 0])\n```\n\nWhere `m` is a head-specific slope that penalizes distant tokens.\n\n### Head-Specific Slopes (Geometric Sequence)\nFor n attention heads, slopes form a geometric sequence:\n```\nm_k = 2^{-8k/n}    for k = 1, 2, ..., n\n```\n\n**Concrete values for 8 heads**:\n```\nm = [1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/16, 1/32, 1/64, 1/128, 1/256]\n  = [2^{-1}, 2^{-2}, 2^{-3}, 2^{-4}, 2^{-5}, 2^{-6}, 2^{-7}, 2^{-8}]\n```\n\n### Design Philosophy\n- Heads with large slopes (1/2): strongly local, syntax-focused\n- Heads with small slopes (1/256): nearly global, semantics-focused\n- Creates a spectrum from local to global attention without explicit multi-scale design\n\n### Comparison with Other Methods\n\n| Property | Sinusoidal | RoPE | ALiBi |\n|----------|-----------|------|-------|\n| Where applied | Added to embeddings | Rotates Q, K | Bias on attention scores |\n| Position info | Additive, pollutes semantics | Multiplicative, preserves norm | Separate from embeddings |\n| Relative position | Via linear transform | Inherent in dot product | Direct distance penalty |\n| Extrapolation | Poor (degrades immediately) | Moderate (with scaling) | Best (2-3x training length) |\n| Training efficiency | Baseline | ~Same | 11% faster |\n| Memory | Baseline | ~Same | 11% less |\n| Inductive bias | None explicit | Decay with distance | Strong recency bias |\n\n### FIRE (Functional Interpolation for Relative positions, Oct 2023, arXiv:2310.04418)\n- Unified framework that can represent T5's RPE, ALiBi, and Kerple as special cases\n- Uses progressive interpolation for better long-context generalization\n\n---\n\n## 10. Mathematical Properties of Good Positional Encodings\n\n### The Five Properties (from HuggingFace analysis)\n\n1. **Uniqueness (Pr1)**: Each position gets a unique encoding, consistent across sequence lengths. Position 5 has the same encoding whether sequence length is 10 or 10,000.\n\n2. **Linear Relation (Pr2)**: The relationship between encoded positions should be mathematically simple. Computing PE(pos+k) from PE(pos) should be straightforward.\n\n3. **Generalization (Pr3)**: Must work for sequences longer than those seen in training.\n\n4. **Deterministic (Pr4)**: Encodings derive from a deterministic, learnable process.\n\n5. **Extensibility (Pr5)**: Must naturally extend from 1D to nD for multimodal data.\n\n### Two Fundamental Factors (Zheng et al., \"Rethinking Positional Encoding\")\n\nThe effectiveness of ANY positional encoding depends on exactly two things:\n\n1. **Stable rank of the embedded matrix**: Higher stable rank = better memorization of training data. The stable rank measures the \"effective dimensionality\" of the position embedding matrix.\n\n2. **Distance preservation**: The encoding should preserve distances between positions in the original space. Better distance preservation = better generalization.\n\n**Fourier features are a special case** that satisfies both conditions. Gaussian basis functions also work. Any shifted basis function satisfying these two criteria will work.\n\n### Additional Desirable Properties\n\n- **Bounded values**: Stay in [-1, 1] for numerical stability\n- **Smooth variation**: Adjacent positions should have similar encodings\n- **No long-term decay**: Distant positions should be as discriminable as nearby ones (this is where RoPE fails)\n- **Relative position sensitivity**: The encoding should make relative distances easy to compute\n\n### How Zeta Zeros Score on These Properties\n\n| Property | Geometric (standard) | Zeta Zeros (proposed) |\n|----------|---------------------|----------------------|\n| Uniqueness | Yes (by construction) | **Yes** (zeros are distinct, ratios irrational) |\n| Bounded | Yes (sin/cos in [-1,1]) | **Yes** (same sin/cos with different frequencies) |\n| Linear relation | Yes (rotation matrices) | **Yes** (same rotation matrix property) |\n| Distance preservation | Moderate (biased by frequency hierarchy) | **Potentially better** (quasi-random spacing avoids systematic bias) |\n| Stable rank | Moderate (geometric = redundant low-freq dims) | **Potentially higher** (irregular spacing = less redundancy) |\n| Long-term decay | **Yes** (known problem, U-shaped attention) | **Potentially reduced** (incommensurable frequencies avoid systematic decay patterns) |\n| Multi-scale | Explicit geometric hierarchy | **Natural** (irregular gaps provide multiple scales without design) |\n\n---\n\n## Key Synthesis: Why Zeta Zeros Might Be Ideal Frequency Bases\n\n### The Argument\n\n1. **The geometric progression in RoPE/sinusoidal is empirically chosen, not mathematically optimal.** The base of 10000 has no theoretical justification. Liu (2026) showed it can be suboptimal.\n\n2. **The \"Lost in the Middle\" problem arises from systematic positional bias** caused by the regular structure of geometric frequency progressions. Ms-PoE partially fixes this with per-head scaling but is still working within the geometric framework.\n\n3. **Zeta zeros are the \"natural frequencies\" of number theory** — they encode the distribution of primes through the quasicrystal/Fourier duality. They have deep mathematical structure but are NOT periodic.\n\n4. **Key mathematical properties of zeta zeros as frequencies**:\n   - **Mutual incommensurability**: Ratios between zeros are (believed) irrational, meaning the resulting rotations never exactly repeat — preventing the aliasing and harmonic artifacts of geometric progressions\n   - **Level repulsion** (GUE statistics): Zeros repel each other, preventing clustering of frequencies — each frequency captures genuinely different positional information\n   - **Multi-scale coverage**: Gaps between zeros vary from ~1.4 to ~6.9 (for first 20), providing natural multi-resolution without explicit design\n   - **Bounded and deterministic**: Same sin/cos functions, so all standard properties (boundedness, smoothness) are preserved\n   - **Quasicrystal structure**: Structured but non-periodic — the mathematical sweet spot between order (too structured = bias) and chaos (too random = no pattern)\n\n5. **The \"Rethinking Positional Encoding\" result** (Zheng et al.) shows that what matters is stable rank and distance preservation, NOT the specific choice of Fourier frequencies. This opens the door to non-geometric frequency bases.\n\n6. **The pair correlation function** `1 - (sin(pi*u)/(pi*u))^2` of zeta zeros means the encoding would have mathematically characterized statistical properties — unlike ad-hoc alternatives.\n\n### Potential Challenges\n\n- **Scaling to d/2 frequencies**: With d_model=768, need 384 frequencies. The first 384 zeta zeros go up to t ≈ 599. Need to verify numerical properties at this scale.\n- **No gradient flow through frequency selection**: Frequencies are fixed (like standard sinusoidal), not learned. But CARoPE shows learned frequencies help — a hybrid approach (zeta-zero initialization + learning) might be optimal.\n- **Computational cost**: Negligible — it's just a different set of constants.\n- **Theoretical gap**: No proof that zeta zeros are optimal; the argument is based on their mathematical properties being well-suited to the task.\n\n---\n\n## Paper References (Complete List)\n\n### Positional Encoding\n1. The plan file has been written to `C:\\Users\\Knack\\.claude\\plans\\dazzling-wiggling-quiche-agent-ae2d63ccfbc72ab8a.md` with full details, but here are the essential findings across all 10 research areas:\n\n---\n\n## Key Findings\n\n### 1. Prime-Based Positional Encodings\n**No published work exists** on using prime-number-based or zeta-zero-based frequency selection for transformer positional encodings. This is a genuinely novel research direction. The closest work is CoPE (Complex Positional Encoding, arXiv:2508.18308) which uses complex-valued embeddings, and CARoPE (arXiv:2507.23083) which uses content-dependent frequencies. The critical theoretical result from \"Rethinking Positional Encoding\" (arXiv:2107.02561) shows that **any shifted basis function** works for positional encoding as long as it has sufficient stable rank and distance preservation -- Fourier is just one special case.\n\n### 2. Complex-Valued Attention\nActive area with recent papers: Holographic Transformers (arXiv:2509.19331) incorporate wave interference into attention; \"Building Blocks for Complex-Valued Transformer\" (ICASSP 2023); CoPE uses complex embeddings where real = semantics, imaginary = position. The key constraint is **Liouville's theorem**: no function can be both bounded and holomorphic over the entire complex plane.\n\n### 3. RoPE Frequency Selection\nRoPE uses `theta_j = 10000^{-2j/d}`, creating a geometric progression of wavelengths from `2*pi` to `10000*2*pi`. **The base of 10000 has NO mathematical justification** -- it is purely empirical. Liu (Feb 2026, arXiv:2602.10959) reinterpreted RoPE as phase modulation on complex oscillators and derived Nyquist-like bounds, showing 10000 can even be suboptimal.\n\n### 4. Lost in the Middle\nLiu et al. (TACL 2024, arXiv:2307.03172) showed 30%+ accuracy degradation when information is in the middle of context. The U-shaped curve is caused by RoPE's long-term decay. Ms-PoE (NeurIPS 2024) partially fixes this with per-head position rescaling (`f(x, m/r) = x * e^{i*(m/r)*theta}`), gaining +3.8 accuracy on Zero-SCROLLS.\n\n### 5. Number Theory and Neural Networks\nThe critical discovery: **zeta zeros form a quasicrystal** (arXiv:2410.03673). Primes placed at `ln(p_n)` produce a structure whose Fourier transform has peaks at the zeta zeros. The Fourier self-duality property (`F[F[chi]](x) = chi(-x)`) forces all zeros to lie on the critical line. Montgomery's pair correlation `1 - (sin(pi*u)/(pi*u))^2` matches GUE random matrix eigenvalue statistics exactly (confirmed by Odlyzko, 1987).\n\n### 6. First 20 Riemann Zeta Zeros (Imaginary Parts)\n```\n14.1347, 21.0220, 25.0109, 30.4249, 32.9351,\n37.5862, 40.9187, 43.3271, 48.0052, 49.7738,\n52.9703, 56.4462, 59.3470, 60.8318, 65.1125,\n67.0798, 69.5464, 72.0672, 75.7047, 77.1448\n```\nGaps range from 1.44 to 6.89 (non-geometric, non-arithmetic). Level repulsion prevents clustering. Ratios are believed mutually irrational/incommensurable.\n\n### 7. Original Sinusoidal PE\n`PE(pos,2i) = sin(pos/10000^{2i/d})`, `PE(pos,2i+1) = cos(pos/10000^{2i/d})`. Wavelengths form geometric progression from `2*pi` to `~62,832`. Relative position encoded via 2x2 rotation matrices. Maximum ~62,832 unique positions.\n\n### 8. Multi-Scale / Fractal Attention\nFasterViT (ICLR 2024) interleaves local + hierarchical attention. GraphFractalNet uses fractal attention constraining attention to recursively clustered subgraphs (sub-quadratic). Ms-PoE assigns different scaling ratios per attention head for multi-scale fusion.\n\n### 9. ALiBi\nAdds static bias `m * [-(i-1),...,-1,0]` to attention scores (no positional embeddings). Head slopes follow geometric sequence `m_k = 2^{-8k/n}`. For 8 heads: `[1/2, 1/4, 1/8, 1/16, 1/32, 1/64, 1/128, 1/256]`. Extrapolates to 2-3x training length. 11% faster training, 11% less memory.\n\n### 10. Properties of Good Positional Encodings\nFive properties: uniqueness, linear relation between positions, generalization to longer sequences, deterministic, extensible to nD. Two fundamental factors (Zheng et al.): **stable rank** of embedding matrix and **distance preservation**. Zeta zeros potentially score well on both: irregular spacing = higher stable rank (less redundancy), incommensurable ratios = better distance preservation.\n\n### Synthesis: Why Zeta Zeros Might Be Ideal\nThe geometric progression in standard PE is unjustified and causes systematic bias (U-shaped attention). Zeta zeros are the natural frequencies of number theory with: mutual incommensurability (no harmonic artifacts), level repulsion (each frequency captures different information), natural multi-scale coverage, and quasicrystal structure (structured but non-periodic).
